## Chapter 6

 

## Crypto Wallets Explained

![](Images/image00317.jpeg)

In the same way that you store physical banknotes and coins in a physical wallet, cryptocurrency wallets are where you store your cryptocurrencies and other digital assets. Using cryptocurrencies as an investment asset is becoming more popular, as is digital payments. If you intend to join this growing trend, you need to be aware of what crypto wallets are and how to use them. This chapter will walk you through the different types of wallets and some tips on choosing the right one for your needs.

![](Images/image00340.jpeg)

What Is a Crypto Wallet?

Crypto wallets are a method of storing your cryptocurrencies, such as Ethereum and Bitcoin, and other digital assets based on the blockchain you may own. These wallets allow you to manage your assets, spend them or use them for trading other cryptocurrencies. Crypto wallets come in two primary types - hardware and software.

There are three types of software wallets - web-based, desktop, and mobile, while hardware wallets are physical devices used to store your crypto securely offline. Let's look at each one.

### Web-Based Wallets

Web-based wallets are simple to use and are typically hosted by crypto exchanges, allowing you to easily buy, store, and sell your cryptos. The wallet provider stores your security keys and manages them. Let's say you use the Coinbase Global exchange to purchase your crypto. In that case, your security keys are stored in a web wallet that always has a connection to the internet - this is known as hot storage. However, this comes with a few security issues. Should the exchange get hacked, you risk losing your assets, for example, and an always-on connection to the internet will always be a risk. However, web wallets provide easy options for managing and purchasing cryptocurrencies. 

### Mobile Wallets

Mobile wallets are simply a piece of software on your tablet or smartphone. Some are not hosted, which means the responsibility for saving your private key and keeping it secure is yours - lose it and you've lost access to your assets. Some of the more popular choices include Coinbase Wallet, Trust Wallet, and Mycelium. Mobile wallets are typically classed as hot storage as you can link them to exchanges, but some provide extra offline security or cold storage. 

### Desktop Wallets

Desktop wallets are similar to mobile wallets and are small pieces of software installed on a desktop computer. Some of the best ones are Exodus, MetaMask, and Electrum, and most offer a choice of hot or cold storage. 

### Hardware Wallets

Hardware wallets are more secure than software wallets, as they allow you to store access to your digital assets on hardware offline. You can connect the wallet to your computer via a USB port to do your transactions and transfers and then disconnect them to keep your access secure in cold storage. This is the highest security level you can get in a crypto wallet, but the responsibility for tracking your security keys is yours. Two of the most popular hardware wallets are Trezor and Ledger

How Do Crypto Wallets Work?

Your cryptocurrencies and other assets are stored on a distributed ledger, and you need the internet to access them. Because of that, if you intend long-term storage of your cryptos, you need to be mindful of the risks of cybersecurity, and setting up your crypto wallet should be your first priority.

Crypto wallets don't physically hold cryptocurrency. Instead, cryptos are stored on the blockchain because they are digital assets, and the blockchain is a distributed digital ledger that stores and manages these assets. Crypto wallets are nothing more than a way of storing the "key" that allows you to access your cryptos.

Features:

*   They give us a long-term method of managing cryptocurrencies.
*   You can store more than one type of cryptocurrency in a wallet.
*   You get a public key to help you do your crypto transactions.
*   Some give you a private key for extra security.

Depending on which wallet you choose, you will likely get two keys - a public and a private key. The public key is created automatically when you set your crypto wallet up and is needed to carry out your transactions. It is much like a web address, and when you make a purchase, accept a crypto payment or send crypto to another wallet, you will need to provide it. The private key is much like a password and is the one key you must keep secure. It is used to manage your cryptos, and unless you choose a hosted wallet option, it is your responsibility to keep the key safe, where no one else can get it.

Using Your Crypto Wallet

When you have decided which wallet to use, you need to set an account up if using a hardware wallet or download the software or app needed for the software wallets. You will be asked to create the security keys you need to keep somewhere safe, and some wallets also offer two-factor authentication for an additional security layer. 

Once your wallet and account are set up, you should transfer the cryptos you have purchased from the exchange to your wallet. Using Coinbase as an example, you will find an option to let you "send" your cryptos. Then you are asked to provide the wallet address and how much crypto to send. 

Most crypto wallets are free to download and offer free crypto storage. However, expect to pay fees for some transactions. Hardware wallets are not free but come in at a reasonable cost - however, it is a small price to pay for security. 

Best Crypto Wallets

I can't recommend using any specific crypto wallet because it is down to your specific needs. You need to consider whether you want one purely for storing your cryptos securely, or are you going to be investing, trading, and doing other transactions? To give you an idea of what's available, I will talk about some of the more popular options available today. It's worth noting that the prices quoted were correct at the time of writing - to get the up-to-date prices, you'll need to visit the relevant websites.

![](Images/image00341.jpeg)

### Trust Wallet

https://trustwallet.com

Trust Wallet is a hot wallet and a mobile app that is free to download and set up. Originally developed by Victor Radchenko, Trust Wallet was acquired in 2018 by Binance and is now the official Binance wallet. 

If you invest in the Binance Coin, the Trust Wallet is required. However, it can also handle many other cryptocurrencies and offers a great many features to ensure excellent security. You get the option of using Google Authentication, Two-Factor Authentication, email or SMS verification, among other features.

While Trust Wallet is a hot wallet, it offers users the security aspects of a cold wallet by storing most of its deposits on a cold storage server. There are no fees for using the wallet; the only fees are associated with transactions on the blockchain, and those are paid to the miners, not Trust Wallet. 

You can make direct crypto purchases via Trust Wallet, although only two exchanges are supported on the platform - Simplex and MoonPay. Alternatively, you can purchase your cryptocurrency form wherever you want and send it to your Trust Wallet. 

### Huobi Pro

[https://www.huobi.com](https://www.huobi.com)

Another free wallet, Huobi Pro is a multi-chain wallet. It is also referred to as a light wallet, but all this means is that the whole blockchain is not downloaded when a transaction is verified; instead, it uses the block headers.

In terms of functionality, Huobi Pro is comprehensive - to some, the interface may seem over-busy. It supports more than a thousand cryptocurrencies, and you have the option of direct purchases on Huobi Global exchange or the P2P market from other traders. It also supports most fiat currencies and is one of the fastest-growing companies globally. 

Huobi Pro offers plenty of security features. While users have sole control over their own private keys, Huobi does offer a guarantee and has previously returned funds to customers where errors have caused loss.

### Coinbase Wallet

[https://wallet.coinbase.com](https://wallet.coinbase.com/)

Founded in 2012, Coinbase is primarily a mobile app. Although they say they are supported on desktops, this is only an extension on the Chrome browser. Coinbase is regulated and licensed in the USA and can be used anywhere in the states except for Hawaii. It is tied to the Coinbase exchange, making it simple to use if you choose Coinbase as your exchange.

Originally only for Bitcoin, Coinbase now supports many cryptos, including Bitcoin Cash, Litecoin, and Ethereum, and more are expected to be made available in the near future.

The app has a simple interface, making it ideal for new wallet users, but their fees are higher than many others. 

### Luno Wallet

[https://www.luno.com/wallet/](https://www.luno.com/wallet/)

Luno is a well-established company with physical offices worldwide. It is ideal for new users and requires nothing more than downloading the app and setting up your account. From there, you can purchase and trade crypto very quickly. Where Luno has a physical presence, you can simply head to a bank and pay funds into a local account. Because Luno is closely tied to some fiat currencies, it is a great choice for people who use online banking for their traditional accounts. 

Luno is both web and mobile-based, with the web access looking identical to the mobile app interface. They don't have wide crypto support just yet, with just five for now - Bitcoin, Litecoin, Ethereum, Bitcoin Cash, and Ripple.

### Exodus

https://www.exodus.com

Daniel Castagnoli and JP Richardson founded Exodus in 2015. More than just a secure wallet, Exodus is trying to bill itself as a crypto wallet for the non-geeks, meaning anyone and everyone can use it easily. It claims to be intuitively designed and tries to simplify an otherwise complex topic. 

Exodus is classed as a hybrid wallet which means it has both a mobile and desktop format. It starts as a basic wallet, allowing you to extend it with the functionality you want using apps, i.e., you can join their rewards system, use live charts, and so on. 

The biggest downside is that the app system is relatively new and isn't as comprehensive as many others we've listed.

### Trezor

[https://trezor.io/](https://trezor.io/)

Trezor is a hardware wallet produced by SatoshiLabs. Hardware wallets are classed as cold storage because they go online only when you connect them to the internet. Some say hardware wallets are for the paranoid but, in reality, they are a much securer way of keeping access to your cryptos safe. 

There are two types of Trezor hardware wallets, the Trezor One costing just under $80 and the Model T costing from $250. They both support multiple cryptocurrencies, including Zcash Litecoin, Bitcoin, Monero, and Dash. You only require two things to set the wallet up - a pin code and recovery seed.  

### Ledger

[https://www.ledger.com/](https://www.ledger.com/)

Ledger is a diverse company, with HQs spread worldwide, and they are one of the more successful companies to produce a hardware wallet. Again, there are two models to choose from, the Nano X starting at $179 and the Nano S, starting from $70. They are incredibly small devices, yet both contain the necessary hardware to support their respective apps. The Ledger devices support more than 1800 cryptocurrencies and work well with many other wallets.

One feature that many people are not happy about is the addition of Bluetooth, given that it isn't the most secure communication method. However, it is possible to disable it.

## Chapter 7

 

## The Different Cryptocurrencies

![](Images/image00317.jpeg)

In 2009, the first digital currency, Bitcoin, was released to much fanfare. At the time, it had little to no competition and reigned supreme, but that would soon change. By 2011, other cryptocurrencies began emerging as the adoption of the underlying technology, the blockchain, began to rise. Today, we have thousands of cryptocurrencies to choose from, and each provides its own unique functions and features. However, most of them are built on the same principles as Bitcoin:

*   No cryptocurrency is regulated, issued, or backed by a bank, government, or any other central authority.
*   They are all created on the blockchain and P2P review.
*   Many crypto coins are encrypted with cryptography.
*   Cryptocurrencies are considered assets and are typically stored in a crypto wallet which can be hardware or software. These allow the crypto owners to manage their coins and trade with them.

As of now, there are upwards of 6-10,000 different coins you can trade in with a total market cap of $2 trillion.

The Different Types of Crypto

Cryptocurrencies are typically split into two categories:

*   Coins, such as Bitcoin and altcoins.
*   Tokens, programmable assets on the platform's blockchain.

The three words - crypto, tokens, coins - are used interchangeably, but you should understand the differences between the three so you understand cryptocurrency. Any blockchain currency that isn't Bitcoin is an altcoin - more about those shortly.

### Coins vs. Tokens

Both tokens and coins are forms of cryptocurrency, but they function differently. A crypto coin is a form of currency built into its blockchain. For example, BTC (Bitcoin) is built on the Bitcoin blockchain, and ETH (Ethereum) is built on the Ethereum blockchain.

Tokens are also built on blockchains, but they are not currency. They are programmable assets that allow users to create smart contracts and execute them. Smart contracts are used to establish who owns specific assets not on the blockchain network. Tokens are also used to represent a value of some sort, such as money, electricity, digital assets, etc., and can be received and sent. As an example, the BAT (Basic Attention Token) is an Ethereum token used in digital advertising. 

### Altcoins

Altcoin is the short form of "alternative to Bitcoin" and is any digital coin other than Bitcoin. Most were created to improve on some parts of Bitcoin, and some examples include Ethereum, Litecoin, USD Coin, Namecoin, and Peercoin. 

Bitcoin is hard-capped, which means only a set number of coins will ever be in circulation, and other cryptocurrencies are the same. This ensures demand and a rise in perceived value. 

Most of today's altcoins share the basic framework Bitcoin was built on, along with some of Bitcoin's characteristics. However, they all provide something different. Some do the same as Bitcoin, producing transaction blocks and validating them, but they have a different process to do it. Others offer brand-new features, such as smart contracts. 

### Tokens

Typically, tokens are created and distributed via an ICO (Initial Coin Offering), which is similar to a traditional stock offering. Tokens may be represented in the following ways:

*   As value tokens, such as Bitcoins.
*   As security tokens, which are much like stocks.
*   As utility tokens, which are for specific use-cases.

All tokens represent a value of some description, but they are not valuable in themselves. In the same way, the paper dollar you have in your wallet may not be worth what it says it is. However, a token can be used to carry out transactions in different things 

The primary way a token is different from a coin is that tokens are constructed on blockchains that already exist for digital coins, such as Ethereum or Bitcoin. 

Top 12 Common Cryptocurrencies
