The only people who will be laughing to the bank are those who have gamed the system because it isn’t as regulated as it’s sure to be in the coming years. However, it would help to remember that several companies remained standing because they actually had value back then. In other words, you don’t want to be too quick to dismiss this space for any reason. Once upon a time, the idea of the internet was mocked, cell phones were deemed useless toys, and computers weren’t considered powerful enough to revolutionize all aspects of life and living. However, here we are.

So, you definitely don’t want to miss out on the numerous opportunities in the crypto space and with NFTs. There will always be a few diamonds in the rough, and this book is written to help you discover what’s worth it and what doesn’t deserve a cent. It’s written in simple English, and all the concepts we will cover here will be explained as simply as possible. By the time you’re done reading, you will feel more comfortable about your investing decisions. Just because Bitcoin has soared astronomically doesn’t mean you can’t still make money. There’s a nice slice of pie for you, too. If you’re hungry, let’s dig in already.

## Chapter 1

 

## Crypto-What?

![](Images/image00317.jpeg)

The world has been going through digitization for quite a while. Once upon a time, we had to listen to music on vinyl, cassette tapes, then CDs. Then someone figured we could listen to music digitally on mp3 players. Once upon a time, you had to go see a play. Then you could watch silent movies, which then became talkies, which then became video cassette tapes, DVDs, Blu Ray, etc. Now all you have to do is open up your preferred app and watch what you want if you don’t feel like cluttering up your home with these things or going to the movies.

![](Images/image00348.jpeg)

So, it only makes sense that at some point in time, the currency with which we do business that keeps our economies running was going to be digital. We’re not at a point where we’ve fully switched over to digital currencies across the world, but the writing is very clear on the wall, written in bold type with screaming red ink. We’re witnessing the birth of a new era, the era of cryptocurrency. Well, technically, we witnessed the birth of this epoch with Bitcoin, but we’re looking at the awkward toddler phase of crypto, and it’s looking good.

What Is Cryptocurrency?

The term “cryptocurrency” is an umbrella term that encompasses all types of digital currencies, from Bitcoin to Ethereum to Litecoin. This means that all cryptocurrency is made up of two different parts: tokens and cryptography. The tokens represent what you can purchase with the currency, such as a certain amount of a product or service. Cryptography is the way that tokens are transferred from one user to another. It also prevents counterfeiting.

For example, imagine you want to pay for a cup of coffee with Bitcoin. You take the Bitcoin out of your wallet and send it to the barista in exchange for your coffee. The transaction is made using a specific type of cryptography to prevent someone from duplicating the token and using it more than once (known as double-spending). The barista can be certain that the token you sent to him is legitimate because of this cryptography. The great thing about cryptocurrency is that it’s super easy to send and receive no matter where you are in the world — even easier than dealing with traditional transfer methods.

The traditional banks will hold onto your money and help you transfer it when needed, but with cryptocurrency, no governing body manages your funds. In other words, you’re the bank. You and millions of others out there. All you need to run your bank is free software that allows you to connect with other people’s phones and computers directly, doing away with the need for middlemen. You can literally become part of the crypto network in a matter of minutes, and all you need to do is download an app on your phone or PC. That’s it. Your money is safe because it is secured by cryptography.

The Blockchain

Cryptocurrencies like Bitcoin and Ethereum are built on a blockchain. A blockchain is a public ledger or database containing all publicly available information about each token. It also contains the information needed to verify that each token is unique and hasn’t been used more than once (i.e., you can’t use the same Bitcoin twice). As mentioned above, cryptography prevents anyone from duplicating your tokens and using them more than once. So how does this really work? Bitcoin’s public ledger contains:

*   Every coin that has ever existed
*   The total amount of coins in existence at any point in time
*   How much those coins can be used to do various things (i.e., spend on certain services, sell)
*   Who owns each coin
*   All mining information is required to make new coins and keep old ones alive.

If the term “blockchain” sounds like it’s really complicated stuff, don’t let it frighten you. The fact is that anyone with Excel skills could easily create their own blockchain. It’s really that simple. Now, to be clear, when you’re talking about the blockchain in terms of cryptocurrency, certain things are particular about it. For one thing, they’re append-only, implying that unlike with a regular spreadsheet where you can edit to your heart’s content, the blockchain can only be added to. You can’t take away information from it, and you can’t alter anything that’s already been added.

Another interesting trait of the blockchain is every block (every entry) within the ledger is connected to the one before it. In other words, the new blocks entered into the database must have a hash of the ones just before them. What is a hash, exactly? That’s a digital fingerprint. Fingerprints are unique and, therefore, cannot be replicated. This is why you can’t spend the same Bitcoin twice, for instance, and why you can’t attempt to game the system by replicating transactions, as the computers on the network wouldn’t verify them without the correct hash. So, each block has a fingerprint that references the one before it, and this is what creates the chain of blocks. Since it’s a tech thing and not a super cool TV show, no one calls it a “chain of blocks.” It’s the blockchain.

Because the blockchain can’t be changed, any attempted changes to the block will affect the fingerprint, affecting all other blocks to come. This means that even if someone were to figure out a way to create those changes, something would obviously have been tampered with. Everyone would know, and it would be easy to trace the origin of the problem.

If you’ve always wondered what the blockchain was and felt it should have been a lot more complex than what you’ve just learned, don’t be disappointed. It’s still a rather innovative concept because the thing is, no matter who you are, you can get access to other people’s blocks by downloading them from the network and building your own copies on your computer that are just the same.

Let’s assume that you and four other people are using the software. You’d like to send ten coins to person A, and so you issue an instruction to that effect to everyone else on the network, but person A doesn’t get the coins right away. At that same point in time, person B might want to send ten coins to person C, and they also issue an instruction to the software’s network. Now, you have two pending instructions that anyone could gather at any point in time to create their own block.

So, the question becomes if any Jane or Joe can create a block, how does the system ensure there’s no room to cheat? Some people have probably thought about creating a block that instructs one person to pay them billions of coins or allows them to buy a 100-million dollar apartment in Van Nuys with money that isn’t theirs. However, it doesn’t work that way. For one thing, cryptography makes that impossible. There’s also the matter of game theory and the proof of work consensus algorithm. These things come together to make it really hard for you to spend money that isn’t yours, and this framework has made it possible for cryptocurrencies to thrive so well.

The Consensus Algorithm

This mechanism makes it possible for every machine or participant on the network to work together in a coordinated and distributed manner. It ensures that everyone on the system deems one particular truth to be valid, even though other participants may fail. This means that the system works because it’s fault-tolerant. The Byzantine fault tolerance or BFT is one trait of the system that makes it possible to deal with failures in the system, as it continues to work even when certain agents or nodes choose to act maliciously or dishonestly. In other words, each participant must choose whether to validate a transaction or not, and once a decision has been made, it shouldn’t and cannot be changed. Also, the participants must choose to come to the same conclusion and carry out their validations in the same way.

When you’re dealing with a centralized system, there’s only one entity in charge, which means they can make whatever changes they want because there’s no one to challenge them, and there are no checks and balances. However, cryptocurrency is founded on the principle of decentralization of control. You’re dealing with a database that’s distributed among many participants, and that begs the question, how do they all agree what blocks or entries should be included in the chain? Enter the consensus algorithms.

The blockchain contains a record of everyone’s balance on that system. Each participant is better known as a node, and each one has an exact copy of the entire database, which is why it’s hard to mess up the records because all the information is synchronized. With public-key cryptography, it’s impossible to spend someone else’s coins, even if you have a record of what they’ve got. However, every node turns to one source to figure out whether the coins in each participant’s balance are still available or if they’ve been spent.

This brings us to the Proof of Work system. What it does is coordinate each node on the network. Before we get into the PoW, let’s take a look at the properties of the consensus algorithms available. To kick things off, it is mandatory that every node (same as the user who acts as a validator, remember) that wants to add an entry or a block must put forth a stake, which is a value that makes it impossible for the nodes to take dishonest actions. When a node chooses to cheat, that node will be dispossessed of its stake. In other words, their reputation goes low as they become untrustworthy, and they could even lose cryptocurrency or processing power, too.

The reason nodes are willing to take this risk is because of the inherent reward, which is usually in the form of the cryptocurrency of that system’s protocol, either as transaction fees or newly minted coins. Transparency matters because it helps every node discover who’s cheating and when. While the production of entries is pretty pricey, it’s often cheap to validate them. In this way, the nodes are forced to be accountable by the regular people using the cryptocurrency.

Proof of Work (PoW) and Proof of Stake (PoS)

Proof of Work is the premier of consensus algorithms that was first put in place in Bitcoin by Nakamoto, but the concept itself is not new. With this one, the validators or miners will take input with variable size and, from that input, carve out output that has a fixed size. They do this with hash functions, which are mathematical formulas. This process is called hashing. So, with PoW, the validators will hash all the input they want to enter onto the blockchain until they come to a fixed conclusion.

The hash itself is an alphanumeric entry that appears random and is created through the hashing mechanism. While it may appear random, it really isn’t because as you run that same data again with a hash mechanism, you will always get the same answer each time. If you dare to change even a tiny aspect of it, you will get a different answer. When you study the output, it’s not easy to tell what the original information that was hashed is, and so this makes the output an excellent way for nodes to demonstrate they were aware of specific sets of data before a set point in time. If you could offer someone the hash first and then show them the data much later, that person will be able to take the hash and pass it through the same function, arriving at the same results.

For Proof of Work, specific conditions must be met before an entry to the chain is considered valid. One such condition could be that the only valid blocks are the ones with a hash that starts with “27.” There’s only one way that a validator could create a bl0ock that matches that condition, and that would be through brute-force, so they continue changing different parameters in the data available to them, which in turn leads to a different result each time. 

This process continues until the miner finally gets the correct hash. This is the basic idea; however, it’s not that simple when it comes to the blockchains that matter. Other miners are trying to crack the code too, which means you’d be competing with them, and you’re going to need access to very special machinery called ASICs before you can create valid blocks. To be clear, mining used to be easy enough to do on your computer, but now you’d need a warehouse full of these machines to get just one block the system deems valid.

To mine successfully, your stake is the power you use to run your ASICs and the price of the machines, which you can’t use for anything other than crypto mining. If you want to get back your money, you’ve got no option but to mine, but the rewards can be well worth it when you can add a new entry to the crypto database. While you might have to run through trillions and trillions of combos just to get the correct hash, the network only needs to use a hashing function to verify your hash once. If it’s valid, you’ll be rewarded. If it isn’t, you lose power and time.

Proof of Stake was created as a viable alternative to the Proof of Work system. There are no miners involved with this consensus algorithm, and there’s no need for too much power or expensive ASICs. You can just use your computer… and then some. Your investment isn’t physical but digital when it comes to this algorithm. It’s the cryptocurrency. Each protocol will have unique rules, but for the most part, there’s an entry-amount of the crypto you must have before you can take part in the staking process.

When you have the funds, the idea is to keep them locked and untouched. You cannot and shouldn’t move them during the staking process, and you and other validators will agree on which transactions will be entered into the blocks to come. In other words, think of it as placing a bet. If the block you’ve placed your bet on is considered the chosen one, you get a cut of the transaction fees depending on how much of the coin you’ve staked. So, the higher your investment, the more profit you’ll make. You can’t game the system by putting forth invalid crypto transactions, though, because you stand to lose some or all of what you’ve staked if you do. This is what drives investors to be honest because it’s in their best interests to do the right thing.

For the most part, there are no newly-minted coins offered to you as a reward for validating transactions. You have to receive the cryptocurrency for that blockchain either by launching the system with Proof of Work before moving on to Proof of Stake or choose to use the initial distribution of thee coins through an Initial Coin Offering (ICO) directly to regular people or an Initial Exchange Offering (IEO), where a Cryptocurrency Exchange acts as a third-party evaluator of the cryptocurrency to be sure it’s actually worth investing in and has a valid utility that could mean an increase in the coin’s value over time.

The less common, smaller coins make use of Proof of Stake, and so it’s still not too clear if PoS could be a better option for transaction validation than PoW. Theory is one thing. Real-life application is another. PoW and PoS are just two of many consensus algorithms. You don’t need to know them all, but it’s important to understand why you don’t have to worry about losing your money in crypto transactions and only need to be concerned with the natural undulations in price that affect every financial asset or instrument across all markets.

Benefits of Cryptocurrency

Transparency: All cryptocurrency transactions are public. This allows anyone to see exactly what is going on with any coin or token in real-time. Any user can send currency to others without trusting a third party. Once they have the cryptography required, they can send and receive currency directly from other users without a middleman.

Consensus: Another benefit is that public ledgers have consensus built into their very core. This makes it very difficult for someone to try to split the community. If one user decides to double-spend the same coin, there won’t be enough of that coin in existence for them to make it happen (there will be a few other users who will see the transaction and refute it).

Resistant to Censorship: Thanks to the design of the network, there’s no way for hackers to force a shutdown of the system and make people lose their money.

Permissionless: No one has the right to keep you from accessing your cryptocurrency or using it the way you want to. When you have assets in the bank or with other traditional bodies, they can freeze those assets whenever they want and for any reason. Cryptos are not like that. You can transact whenever you want, and there are no limits on how much you can send and receive.

Speed and Cost: Sending cryptocurrency instead of cold, hard cash is ridiculously fast and just as cheap. By the time you get your money using a regular international wire transfer, you’ll find that you’re several short dollars. But with cryptocurrency, the fees are dramatically lower, and it’s always nice to know you saved a little extra, isn’t it?

However, there are some drawbacks. It’s hard for a younger person to use cryptocurrency (for example, you can use credit cards without proving your identity). Although you can use fake information to buy cryptocurrency, it’s quite a hard process to do so. With time, it will be easier for people to use cryptocurrency.

Cryptocurrency is not easy to understand. It uses cryptography and math that are considered completely inaccessible by some people. This isn’t necessarily a bad thing since that means you can’t break into the system (it is a public ledger, after all). But in the future, it will be more accessible and easier for people to understand how it works. However, I would make it a point to spend time educating people on the basics of cryptocurrency and blockchain technology.

## Chapter 2

 

## Bitcoin and Ethereum

![](Images/image00317.jpeg)

In the Beginning, There Was Bitcoin

You may wonder what the differences are between one cryptocurrency and another. The fact is, with the sheer number of cryptocurrencies out there, it’s going to be extremely difficult to list all those differences in detail. You should know that some of them are more private than others. Some work faster than others when it comes to transaction speeds. Also, some of them are much easier to program than others. When you’re looking into a particular cryptocurrency, it’s important to DYOR, which means do your research. Doing your research is a very good rule of thumb when it comes to investing your hard-earned money in general. In this chapter, we’re going to focus on Bitcoin.

![](Images/image00349.jpeg)

Bitcoin has experienced several dramatic rises and falls ever since 2008. It’s decentralized, working with peer-to-peer (P2P) exchanges on electronic networks, doing away with the need for a third party like a bank or the government. Now, this may be the world’s first crypto, but it’s not perfect. However, it’s a wonderful instrument for speculation at the very least, which means you can trade it against the dollar or euro to make some money from the differences between the crypto and fiat currencies.

The Beginning of Bitcoin

Who is the genius behind the creation of the very first cryptocurrency, Bitcoin? No one knows. They could be a person or a group or an intergalactic space being for all we know. What we do know is that they call themselves Satoshi Nakamoto. In 2008, they had put out a document of 9 glorious pages that described how Bitcoin would work as a system. Several months later, the world was gifted with this software in 2009. Since then, it has inspired other cryptocurrencies in its wake, with some using the same framework and others choosing to do things differently.

2008 was the year of the Great Recession. People had learned the hard way that banks and other financial institutions couldn’t be trusted. Ditto for governments, too. So, the time was ripe to address the issues surrounding control of money and resources, and Bitcoin was the perfect answer at the time. January 3, 2009, was a significant day in the history of cryptocurrency. That was the day the genesis block — the very first block on the blockchain — was mined. A week after that, as the premier test transaction. In the earlier months after Bitcoin’s arrival, it could only be gotten through the process of mining. It had no actual value at the time, and there wasn’t an actual transaction after the test one until over a year later. A man in Florida ordered two Papa John’s pizzas, costing him $25 at the time, which was about 10,0000 BTC. This was on the 22nd of May 2010. If only he’d known how much those 10,000 coins would have netted him! This transaction set the financial rate for Bitcoin’s value. For a penny, you could have 4 Bitcoins. As of the 12th of November, 2021, this would have been $689,990,000. That’s definitely going to get you several lifetimes of pizzas. Speaking of which, May 22 is now called Pizza Day by the crypto community.

In its early days, all transactions with Bitcoin would be used in barter on the internet, and that meant the value was arbitrarily set. By 2011, those with coding skills and the capacity to mine went on to create other cryptocurrency networks, specifically Ethereum

And Litecoin. The goal was to build something better based on Bitcoin’s blockchain code so that it could have a variety of uses. Because the utility was becoming broader, more and more people hopped onto the crypto bandwagon, which was part of what made Bitcoin seem even more valuable to the masses and that certain businesses decided to transact with this crypto.

By 2010, Bitcoin was listed on exchanges, which meant it was so much easier for the average person to get in on trading and investing it. The exchanges helped Bitcoin finally get a price that you could rely on. It was especially good that it was pegged against the greenback, and in El Salvador, they even use Bitcoin and the dollar as their official currency.

Bitcoin’s Price Journey

Bitcoin is a very volatile asset. This volatility is what attracts millions of speculators worldwide to anticipate the price changes and see how they can profit from that. In spite of the volatility, it would appear that no matter how low it drops, the only way is up — or at least that appears to have been the case since 2009. The thing about Bitcoin is that historically, it’s had a habit of growing in leaps and bounds only to suddenly and dramatically retrace in price. Bitcoin hit the $1 mark as of February 2011, and for some years after that, it held steady at just beneath $2. The first Bitcoin bubble was in June 2011, when the price had shot up to $31, only to shoot back down to being worth only one digit.

Bitcoin then peaked again, hitting the $200-mark in April 2013. By November 2013, it had hit the $1,000-mark. Four years after that, also in November, Bitcoin shot up to $10,000. In 2017, critics were warning that the asset was in a bubble, and stubborn bulls decided the premium prices were worth paying to own Bitcoin. From 2017 to 2018, many ICOs were being held that added to the bubble, causing a flood of billions of dollars to flow into cryptocurrencies. The FUD (Fear, Uncertainty, and Doubt) hit hard in early 2019, leading to the prices tumbling down. After this fall, institutions like banks were legally allowed to own Bitcoin. So they came into the space, along with other players that have taken steps to make sure the crypto market is a viable and stable one with fidelity that allows traders and investors to be even safer than ever before. In November 2021, Bitcoin hit $68,990. As of the time of this writing, Bitcoin sits around the $39,000-mark.

Something in the Ether…

Ether is the cryptocurrency that is native to the Ethereum network, which came up to offer Bitcoin some competition with different properties, tech, and aims. It’s a decentralized blockchain network like Bitcoin, except that the Ether token does a lot more than just let the users transact. If you’ve got Ether, you can make some extra moneyin several ways, including:

*   Staking
*   Creating
*   Storing and using your own non fungible tokens or nfts (we’ll talk about these soon, promise)
*   Trading cryptos
*   Interacting with social media
*   Playing your favorite video games

and so on. Ethereum is considered the Web 3.0, which is basically the most advanced version of the web as we know it, offering support for:

*   Decentralized Exchanges (DEXs)
*   Decentralized Finance (DEFi)
*   Decentralized Applications (DApps)

The Humble Beginnings of Ethereum

This crypto project wasn’t always as prominent or recognized as it is now. Created by Vitalik Buterin, it was meant to be the solution to the many issues Bitcoin was fraught with. In 2013, Buterin had put out its white paper 2013, in which he explained the idea behind smart contracts. These contracts work like “if-then” statements, and they’re immutable and automated as well, making it possible to create DApps. Sure, DApps were already being developed in the blockchain industry, but the problem was the platforms they were run on couldn’t run on anything other than their native systems. So, for Buterin, he thought it was important to have a network that would bring in the interoperability feature and consequently bring actual utility to the crypto space. This is what led to the creation of Ethereum 1.0.

![](Images/image00350.jpeg)

Consider this version of Ethereum to be just like the App Store: It’s a single space, but it hosts numerous apps that have to stick to the same rules when it comes to coding. Everyone coding an app has to do so working within the confines provided by Apple, but they have the liberty to get creative with their own rules as long as they remain within that framework. In the case of Ethereum, there’s no ruling body insisting on controlling the rules and regulations. Remember, this is a decentralized system, and therefore, the power lies with the people.

Buterin founded Ethereum with Amir Chetrit, Anthony Di Lorio, Charles Hoskinson, Gavin Wood, Mihai Alisie, and Jeffrey Wilcke. They had to presell tokens to get up to $18,439,086 in funding for projects they would need to create at the moment and later on. They are also responsible for creating Switzerland’s Ethereum Foundation meant to help maintain the network and come up with innovations. Since Buterin had intended for it to be a nonprofit foundation, some of his partners opted out.

With time, developers got in on the game with their own concepts around decentralization. They created the DAO — the decentralized autonomous organization — which was responsible for deciding what would happen with the network. They had the power of a smart contract, which meant they didn’t need a CEO to rule all things Ethereum-related. If there were to be an amendment to the network, it would only be implemented if the majority voted in favor of that change. This was all fine and dandy until the legendary DAO heist, where a hacker stole a whopping $40,000,000 from the DAO by exploiting a security loophole. The only thing the DAO could do was to opt to have a hard fork, leaving behind the old Ethereum network. Think of it like them basically updating the network in the same way you would an antivirus. The OG network is now known as Ethereum Classic, while the new one is still called Ethereum.

The Inner Workings of Ethereum

The Ethereum network is like Bitcoin in that it’s on thousands and thousands of nodes, keeping it safe from security attacks or being taken offline completely. Ethereum is what powers the Ethereum Virtual Machine or EVM, which is a computer. This computer is backed up by every node, meaning that when there are transactions or other interactions on the network, every node has to verify them and make the necessary changes to their own backups.

The transactions are saved to the entries on Ethereum’s blockchain, and like with Bitcoin, the validators must make sure the entries are valid before adding them to the network. The mining process of choice here is the PoW, and every entry has a fingerprint made up of 64-digits — all unique. The miners try to discover the code and show that it really is one of a kind before they can be rewarded with Ether or ETH. Every transaction on this network has a fee, popularly called gas. If you’re planning on sending someone some Ether, you have to pay that fee to the validators, who then do their work. This is why the miners don’t mind continuing to mine and make sure the system remains secure because they get paid to do that. Also, the gas curbs how many actions you can take for each transaction you make, and this stops nefarious players from spamming the network.

One thing that makes the cryptocurrency Ether different from Bitcoin is that it has actual utility, while the latter only has value. ETH is infinite in supply, and new coins are minted through mining. As Ethereum goes on to the Proof of Stake consensus algorithm, it will also offer rewards through staking. Theoretically speaking, the demand for Ether isn’t likely to drop, and that means we shouldn’t have to worry about it losing its value as fiat currency does due to inflation.

The issue with Ethereum is that the gas fees can be ridiculous depending on what’s happening with the network. Each block on the blockchain only has a limited amount of gas that comes down to how large the transactions are. For this reason, the miners are often more interested in the transactions that have the most expensive gas fees. This means that every user on the network competes to be the first one to validate transactions. In competing with one another, the gas fees soar gastronomically — pun intended — which, during peak periods, congest the network. This is one of the reasons Ethereum 2.0 is being worked on.

If you want to do anything at all on the Ethereum network, you need Ether, which you keep in a digital wallet that allows you to connect to all the DApps you want. Think of that wallet as a passport allowing access to any part of the Ethereum nation. It’s basically like the internet, except you have to pay for stuff, and no centralized parties are selling your personal information for profit. You have cryptocurrency in place of your private data, which means all your interactions and transactions can remain anonymous. There’s never any reason for you to experience discrimination on account of how much you’ve got or who you are.

Decentralized Finance — Ethereum’s Crown Jewel

This is the most amazing achievement of this network to date. There are many DApps with just as many utilities, and they continue to become more widespread. The more users adopt them, the more utility Ethereum has, and the biggest space where you can see this rapid adoption and expansion is Decentralized Finance. For instance, if you’re an artist, you could theoretically make several millions of dollars with your work easier than ever before. All you have to do is put your work on the blockchain. How? Through Non Fungible Tokens.

It may make no sense because before crypto was a thing, you could just “right click and save” any image that catches your fancy on the internet, and you would have a copy just like that. In fact, you can still do that with any digital art sold as NFT. So why bother paying through the nose when you can take a screenshot? The thing is that ownership is what really matters here. NFTs offer digital art collectors proof of ownership and act as a store of value. So, it’s pretty easy for collectors to see why they’d be happy to pay for digital art instead of downloading it for free.

Think of it this way: Say you have an original by Picasso. That would naturally be worth a lot more than all the copies and forgeries out there, but only once you can prove that you’ve got the real McCoy and other experts can verify that. The real art piece would be the NFT, while the copies would be the same as those images “right-clicked and saved as.” NFTs can also be used as accessories and other things in different online environments and games. You can, for instance, buy a Beeple painting and hang it up in one of your houses in, say, Skyrim. Or you could outfit your game character, say a knight, with NFT sneakers by Nike. We’ll talk more about NFTs later, but for now, let’s talk about how to invest in crypto.

## Chapter 3

 

## Ripple and Other Alts

![](Images/image00317.jpeg)

In this chapter, we’re going to take a look at other altcoins, which are basically all coins that aren’t Bitcoin (and some would say, Ethereum). The “alt” in altcoins represents alternatives. The very first one was Namecoin, but today it’s really not that relevant, so we’re not going to talk about that here.

![](Images/image00351.jpeg)

Ripple XRP

Ripple is a different beast altogether, but many believe it is massively undervalued at this time due to various regulatory issues, among other things. Most people assume that Ripple is the same as XRP, the cryptocurrency itself. That’s not the case, though the company does business and has its ecosystem running with XRP. Where you can mine other cryptos on the Bitcoin and Ethereum networks, you can’t do that with XRP because the number of coins in circulation is capped at 100 billion.

XRP was released to the public in 2012, and it is the invention of Ripple Labs Inc. The validation process of all transactions happens using independent servers. Early in April 2021, Ripple hit an all-time high price of above a dollar, and this was in spite of the concerns surrounding the company and the coin since the government has been slowing things down for Ripple and XRP. You can exchange this crypto for any other one you want, and the great thing about XRP is that it is much faster than many others out there.

Speed is the main problem Ripple wants to fix. While everything has picked up speed in so many ways thanks to the internet over the past few decades, banking transactions are needlessly slow and cumbersome, especially when it comes to moving money across the globe. You might wonder how XRP is different from other cryptos when it comes to solving this issue, and you’d be right to ask. The thing is Ripple intends to do it much better than anyone else, working with not just the coin but the XRP Ledger as well, both of which, again, is not tied to the company.

The Origin Story

Software developer Ryan Fugger came up with RipplePay in 2004, a platform meant to help transfer money. The current state of the company Ripple is as a result of this one platform and its evolution over the years, as well as the influence of several people like David Schwartz, Arthur Britto, and Jed McCaleb (who founded Mt. Gox), all of the engineers who realized it was smart to think outside the Bitcoin box and come up with a better way to crypto, so to speak. Their decision to work together on this led to the XRP Ledger, and in 2012, itwent live.

In 2014, McCaleb went on to become a co-founder of Stellar, with its cryptocurrency being XML. This meant he no longer was part of Ripple, and now it’s Chris Larsen who’s the executive chairman of the board of directors at Ripple. In 2017, Brad Garlinghouse became the CEO, just after Larsen chose to leave that spot in 2016. 

In 2018, XRP hit a very important high at $3 for each coin, pushing up from a remarkable low of $0.05 in 2017. December 2020 saw the United States Securities and Exchange Commission (the SEC) laying a complaint against Ripple for selling XRP to US investors because the company was able to raise $1.3 billion from the sale of these coins, which the SEC didn’t consider to be officially recognized securities. However, a group who had purchased XRP came forward to point out that the coin ought to be classified as anything but security. Ripple itself pointed out that the SEC moved in a little too late, long after the other US governing bodies had deemed the coin something other than a security.

Ripple’s Goal

Whenever you need to make international transactions, the banks have to work with the Society for Worldwide Interbank Financial Telecommunications or SWIFT to process all transactions. This has always worked, but the trouble is that operational costs must be accounted for, and that’s part of why you don’t get exactly what you were sent. Ripple as a company is all about looking for efficiency in financial transactions, looking to get rid of the unnecessary costs, and actually settling all transfers in real-time. They hope to achieve this while maintaining transparency in transfers than other methods and bolstering the security of the old-school banks’ use. As far as the company is concerned, if you can send a text message to someone and they can get it as soon as you hit the send button, the same should apply with money.

As part of Ripple’s solutions, they have:

*   The XRP Ledger
*   RippleX
*   RippleNet
*   XRP itself

The XRP Ledger is similar in concept to the blockchain, and it’s open-source as well. The ledger is what allows the cryptocurrency to be fully functional. Again, both the ledger and the coin function on their own without the company’s influence. This ledger, XRPL, is like the Ethereum blockchain, allowing people to create whatever solutions they need. You can also trade this coin if you want to.

RippleX is solely dedicated to producing solutions to financial issues using the blockchain, which anyone interested can use to transfer money. The advantage of this is that rather than having to come up with your own software or solutions, you can make use of the tools offered by RippleX.

RippleNet is a network that allows financial bodies to connect with one another to send and receive money faster. Since there’s less fragmentation here, the transactions are cheaper and faster than the older infrastructure banks had in place. RippleNet is the combination of the former xVia, xCurrent, and xRapid.

The XRP Ledger in Detail

Unlike Bitcoin and Ethereum’s blockchains, the XRPL doesn’t use Proof-of-Work or Proof-of-Stake consensus algorithms. Instead, it has the XRPL Consensus Protocol to validate all financial transactions and XRP balances. Unlike the other forms of consensus, the XRPL is by far one of the most efficient.

As with Bitcoin and Ethereum, the ledger stays up and running thanks to the individual users. Before you can successfully send or receive XRP, these individual users who act as validators must collectively agree that all financial processes being performed are valid. In other words, they must come to a consensus. Bitcoin has its blocks of information, and XRPL’s answer to that is “ledgers.” Each of them has very specific information that connects to a previous one. All changes have to be agreed to by a fixed number of users. Where Bitcoin can take as long as 10 to 15 minutes before validating a block, XRPL only needs five seconds, tops.

The Inner Workings of RippleNet

Think of this as a network encompassing the whole world, working based on a set of rules, allowing people to interact financially with one another more easily than ever. It has the On-Demand Liquidity or ODL, which is very critical to improving the speed of transactions when transferring funds across borders because it does away with the need to get pre-funding in place. So, let’s say you’re in America, and you’d like to send some money over to Ukraine, where they use the Hryvnia as their fiat currency and not the US Dollar. With XRP as a middleman between the Hryvnia and the dollar, the ODL makes it possible for you to send your dollars without having to convert it to Hryvnia first. The recipient gets their money in Hryvnia instead of in dollars, doing away with the need for any conversions on their part.

Why Ripple XRP Is Worth Investing In

No Middleman: With Ripple, you don’t have to deal with any middleman that would create more costs and slow down transactions. All clearing processes are automated as well. Compare that to regular, centralized financial institutions, which need to work with insurance companies, clearing houses, and each country’s central bank.

Speed: XRP transactions only take seconds. There is no way that regular banks could possibly beat that time. In fact, depending on where you’re sending or receiving money from, traditional transactions can take as long as one to two days — business days, even. The transfers happen in real-time, and you can track them easier than you can track traditional transactions, on account of them using way too many intermediaries.

Cheap Costs: If you’re going to use traditional banks to do your transfers, you’ll likely be charged anywhere from $25 to 0.5 or 1.4 percent of the transaction amount. But with XRP, you pay as low as $0.01 for each transaction.

What to Watch Out For

While Ripple is set to take over from SWIFT in time, it seems you need to beware of the fact that it’s going to take a fair bit of time and a lot of regulations that need to be clarified before we make the transition to the new world of banking. Also, you need to keep in mind that Stellar, among other competitors, is giving Ripple a run for their money.

Stellar

The token for Stellar is known as Lumens, with the ticker symbol XLM. Stellar was launched in 2014 to help those parts of the world that don’t have access to financial and banking opportunities. This makes Stellar unique because the norm is to take banking to places that already have the infrastructure and enough economic wealth for that. However, Stellar wants to make it easy to transact with companies and people in these underserved and impoverished areas. When it was launched, there were 100 billion Lumens. 95 percent of the Lumens were given out, and the founders held on to 5 percent.

The Stellar Network is an open-source protocol for exchanging money, developed by Jed McCaleb and Joyce Kim. Right from the start, it was designed to facilitate payments between people in any pair of currencies and allow for exchange rates that adjust with demand. You can even send lumens (XLM) to someone who doesn’t have them, then withdraw your own currency or have them withdraw theirs later on when they’re ready. That takes care of the problem of converting currencies to allow you to avoid losing money through unfavorable rates or fees. It’s a process known as “pegging,” and it’s what allows currencies to be pegged to fiat currencies, such as the US dollar or British pound.

Benefits of the Stellar Network

*   Operating off an emulated global network of servers, not a single computer, allows the Stellar Network to process hundreds of transactions per second compared to Bitcoin’s seven per second.
*   Sending payments worldwide using low fees via a novel payment routing system called “InstantSend.”
*   Being able to handle large amounts of money without requiring extra storage space or processing power (unlike with Bitcoin).
*   A fast and secure system for trading Lumens.
*   Eliminating the need for a trusted central authority to maintain order.

The Stellar Network has found success with a wide range of financial institutions, startups, and individuals. Organizations like IBM, Airbus, Stripe, and Ripple use it in their own payment systems. Current projects include Interstellar, which is developing protocols for the international transfer of digital assets such as cryptocurrency, and Goodomy, which provides a way for people to donate their money to charitable entities without donating directly to them.

Litecoin

Litecoin is a digital currency that is based on Bitcoin, with which it shares many similarities. But despite this shared heritage, Litecoin has taken a different path than its older sibling. Indeed, from day one, Litecoin has been designed to be faster, lighter, and cheaper than Bitcoin in the long run. Here we will provide you with a comprehensive overview of the history and fundamentals of Litecoin.

Litecoin was created by former Google engineer Charlie Lee in 2011. His reason for creating this as a sub-project of Bitcoin was because he felt that mobile phone use would become increasingly prevalent throughout the world. He also wanted to expand the market for Bitcoin by making it cheaper and easier to access.

Since its inception, Litecoin has been extremely popular within the cryptocurrency community. In fact, with a market capitalization of over $8 million and a per-unit price of $18, Litecoin is currently trading much higher than its older sibling.

Benefits of Litecoin

Faster Transaction Times: One of the biggest criticisms of Bitcoin is that it takes a long time to complete transactions. This is especially true when dealing with small amounts, such as buying a cup of coffee. In fact, the average transaction time for Litecoin is currently 2.5 minutes. Although it’s not instantaneous, this wait time is three times faster than Bitcoin transactions.

Cheaper to Use than Bitcoin: Another problem with Bitcoin is its high transaction costs. Due to its large network size, Bitcoin transactions are not as scalable as they could be. In fact, it can be difficult to send small amounts to other users in the Bitcoin community. This is because there is a limited supply of Bitcoins compared to the number of people who want to use them.

This problem has been solved by Litecoin, which features faster transaction times, more transactions per second, and lower fees. This means that Litecoin is much easier for people who are not actively using Bitcoin but still want the benefits of digital currency.

Privacy and Security: Another key feature of Litecoin is its privacy and security features. Unlike Bitcoin, which has a rather public ledger showing every transaction, Litecoin uses ‘transaction encryption.’ This means that anyone who wants to use Litecoin don’t need to worry about being spied on.

Instead, all their transactions can be kept completely private and secure. This is because only the recipients of the Litecoins know their identities and addresses. However, if you wish to make public information available, then it is possible to do so by broadcasting a transaction through your wallet.

A Better Scalability Solution: Unlike Bitcoin, when you send a certain amount of LTC, there is no limit on how large an amount can be. This means that users can send as many transactions as they want in a single block. This means that Litecoin is much more scalable than Bitcoin, ensuring that it has lower transaction fees than Bitcoin.

A Longer Timeline: Since Litecoin uses the same hashing algorithm as Bitcoin, every 2.5 minutes, a new Litecoin block is created. If you compare this to Bitcoin, then you will see that the lifespan of the Bitcoin block creation process is approximately every 10 minutes. This means that miners receive their coins quicker, and the network is more responsive with faster confirmations than using Bitcoin.

This unique feature means that transactions are processed faster, and confirmation times are shorter. In fact, Litecoin can go as far as 4.4x faster than Bitcoin. Because it’s the same hashing algorithm and the same POW (Proof of Work) consensus mechanism that Bitcoin uses, Litecoin is designed to be more secure, with an estimated hash rate of over 750 MH/s. Together with these features, transaction speed makes it easier to use than Bitcoin.

More Efficient Mining: Another key difference between Litecoin and Bitcoin is that it can be mined much more efficiently than Bitcoin. This has a number of advantages, such as quicker confirmation times. Litecoin mining requires much less power than Bitcoin mining, meaning that you don’t need to spend so much money on electricity costs.

Litecoin also enables GPU mining, meaning that anyone with a video card or graphics card can mine LTC with minimal effort. This is because Litecoin keeps network blocks smaller than Bitcoin’s, which means less work needs to be carried out by miners to achieve the same hashing power.

## Chapter 4

 

## Before You Trade  
or Invest in Crypto

![](Images/image00317.jpeg)

Trading vs. Investing: What’s the Difference?

The cryptocurrency space is a hot topic with many moving parts. However,the most important distinction to keep in mind when trading and investing in this area is that trading requires an active commitment every time you enter or exit a trade, whereas investing does not. 

A trader decides which type of trade they want to make and how they want to execute it by either buying at a specific price point or selling at a certain price point. A trader always has a risk/reward trade-off and cannot know the true value of their investment until they exit the trade and assess their trade’s total value.

An investor buys cryptocurrency expecting it to maintain or increase in value over time, usually once they solidify the market order. An investor does not have an option to exit a trade until it is liquidated.

For example, if a trader is in the middle of buying some altcoins, they may elect to liquidate their position and enter the next trade. As long as the price of cryptocurrency rises high enough, it will be profitable to buy more coins with that profit. However, sometimes a trader can’t liquidate their position because there isn’t enough time for them to wait for the market to go through their order books and take them back up on the right side of their trade. The next order they could place may not be enough of a profit to make it worth their while.

When you are trading, you can’t see the true value of your investment until you liquidate the trade. If you buy at a high price and sell at a low price, you will lose money. If you buy at a low price and sell at a high price, then you will make money. How much money? You won’t know until after the trade has been liquidated.

If you are an investor, you will know the true value of your investment each time you buy or sell. You can always see the current price of cryptocurrency and estimate what it will be if you hold it to expiration. You won’t have to worry about getting out of a trade before the price goes against you and taking a loss. This is the main distinction to consider when deciding whether you want to trade cryptocurrency or invest in it.

Plan for Success

Why should you have a plan in place before you trade or invest in cryptocurrency?

The cryptocurrency market is unpredictable, and it can be tempting to jump in without any preparation. But just like any other type of investment, cryptocurrencies are risky — and without proper preparation, you could find yourself at a major disadvantage.

In a perfect world, you would have a crystal ball that could tell you where cryptocurrency prices are headed. But since that’s not possible, it’s essential to have a solid plan in place before you make your first transaction. Your plan will depend on the trading or investing strategy you choose to work with. We’ll take a look at different strategies later.

Getting prepared ahead of time can help you avoid common mistakes that make investing in cryptocurrency riskier than it needs to be. This can help you avoid obstacles like not taking advantage of low prices or emphasizing short-term factors when it comes to trading. There are plenty of benefits to having a plan in place before you make your first purchase. For example, a plan can help you understand the cryptocurrency market and make sense of developments in the space.

Although there’s no right way to create a cryptocurrency plan, it’s essential to have one in place before you make your first transaction. It can be tempting to dive in without a plan of your own, but the most successful cryptocurrency investors and traders have come up with their own plans before adding any currency to their portfolios. And while it’s possible that these plans might change over time, having one in place can help you stay on track when it comes to cryptocurrency investing.

What should your cryptocurrency plan include? Your cryptocurrency plan should include every detail of your investing strategy, including how much money you’re going to invest and when that money will be added to a digital wallet. The more specific you can be, the better. For example, you should include the exact amount of cash that you’re planning to spend and when that money will be added to your portfolio.

Ideally, you should also create a completely automated cryptocurrency plan. With this type of automation, it’s possible to add money while doing other things like having lunch at your desk or taking a shower.

How can a cryptocurrency plan help? Creating a cryptocurrency plan before you make your first transaction can help you avoid three common mistakes :

*   Not knowing your entry point: Making an impulse buy without a solid plan in place is a big mistake, and it can lead to losses if you buy on the wrong side of the market. The first step to having an effective cryptocurrency plan is knowing where you’re going to invest and when that investment will take place.
*   Not preparing for price drops: The cryptocurrency market is unpredictable, and you could buy at an all-time high. Purchasing at the wrong time could cause your investment to go down significantly before it starts to climb back up. However, having a cryptocurrency plan in place will help you purchase during the dips to avoid this kind of mistake.
*   Not knowing how much to spend: It’s tempting to spend as much as possible when you’re excited about making your first investment. But it’s much better to be patient when you’re just starting out. By following a plan, you’ll be able to determine how much money you want to spend and prepare for that amount ahead of time.

Here’s how to create your own cryptocurrency plan:

1.  Determine the amount of money you want to invest: The first step is to know how much cash you’ll commit before buying any coins. While there’s no right or wrong amount to spend, you should have a goal in mind. For example, if you’re investing $100, you should know that your final goal will be around $1,000.
2.  If you’re a trader, determine how much money you’re willing to lose per day, week, or month: This is a very important step, so you don’t gamble it all away on tough trading days or weeks.
3.  Determine the time frame for your investment: The timing of an investment can also play a major role in how successful it is. If you’re investing $100 and want to invest it all in the next few days, that will only give you a small return. But if you’re willing to wait a few weeks, you’ll get more bang for your buck, especially if your analysis shows there’s likely to be a big move in your favor.
4.  Determine your entry point: Finally, it’s essential to know where you’ll enter the market. This can be based on price dips or other indicators that you’re working within your chosen strategy. If you enter at a lower price point than $100, your initial investment will go further.
5.  Figure out your exit point as well: Greed is a great way to get slaughtered when trading cryptocurrency. Don’t ever get taken in by the idea that “It could go even further up!” Just cash out where you planned to, and learn to appreciate that you don’t always have to have a trade or an investment going all the time.
6.  Review your plan: Once you have a plan, there’s no need to keep it in a digital format. Instead, most investors write their plans down and keep them in a safe place within view so they can remember what their goals are. You should also review your plan regularly. Once you have a plan in place, there’s no need to review it every day or week. 

However, you should be sure to check in on your investment at least once per month so that you can make any necessary adjustments.

Running Backtests and Forward Tests

Trading stocks and cryptocurrencies is a tricky business. You can make big gains, but you also risk losing your whole investment if the market goes against you. Successful traders know that proper research is the key to this business. Understanding how to backtest and paper trade (trading with a demo account instead of real dollars) is essential in making sure that any system or strategy has been adequately tested and will work for you in the future. Without proper research and testing, you are taking a big risk when investing in new markets and new strategies.

What’s the difference between a backtest and a forward test? Backtesting involves working with historical data to see how your trading strategy would have played out in the past. Forward testing, however, is about working with present price data as it plays out on a live chart. So, it stands to reason that forward testing will take a lot longer than backtesting. 

When it comes to investing and trading, understanding the intricacies of your strategy is crucial. This includes testing certain aspects of your system before actually placing trades. Through backtesting and paper trading, you can better understand your performance to identify potential weaknesses in your strategy and other potential issues that may arise over a long period of time.

Backtesting is a process by which traders essentially test their trading strategy through historical data. This is essentially where the strategy is studied in the past to better predict its performance in the future. This means that the performance of your strategy will be evaluated on past data. Backtesting gives traders an idea of how they can expect to perform if they were to use that system on future data.

One of the most important factors when it comes to backtesting is that it makes a trader aware of the performance of their system in different market conditions. If your strategy performs well in one particular economic environment, backtesting will also tell you its performance in different market conditions.

Backtesting allows a trader to make adjustments to their system before trading becomes live. Once you have conducted accurate backtesting, you can then proceed to paper trading with a far better understanding of your strategy’s potential strengths and weaknesses.

Why Backtest?

The main reason why many traders choose to backtest before trading live is because there is no guarantee that their strategies will work in a live environment. Backtesting allows you to identify the problems with your strategies before putting more capital at risk. This reduces the risk of potentially losing more money than you may have originally planned.

Paper trading involves paper copies of your strategy being traded against other similar strategies and over different time periods. By using statistics, you can then compare your performance against similar systems. This can prove extremely helpful in identifying strengths and weaknesses in your trading system.

Although backtesting can be a very useful tool, it is important to note that you should only begin using it if you are confident that your strategy will work in the future. If there is any doubt or lack of confidence, don’t start down the path of backtesting.

Before discussing how you could go about backtesting, it is important to understand what trade means. There are three main components in any trade: a maker, a taker, and the trade price. The maker is the person who wishes to make a trade with another entity. The taker represents the entity on the other end of that trade willing to buy or sell an asset at a particular price. Finally, you have the price of the asset being traded, which can be any value between 0 and 1.

To backtest your strategy, you first need to create an indicator that will effectively test your strategy’s performance. This is essentially the core of your trading system. The indicator needs to be able to adapt based on those three trade variables mentioned above.

To make sure that your indicator is working correctly, you need to fine-tune it. This will improve its performance in the future. The indicator should be able to work effectively with a large range of values. Ideally, the indicator will adapt well and respond accordingly to any changes in these variables.

Another point is that your trading strategy needs to be flexible. If it is not flexible, then it may fail on live trade, which could lead you to lose money that you had planned on making otherwise. As we are talking about backtesting, you will need to be very careful about the values and settings of your indicator. If a large change happens in your trading strategy’s performance, you should ensure that your indicator will also adapt accordingly.

How to Backtest

Once you have created your strategy and chosen an indicator that is capable of working with a wide range of values, it is time to start backtesting. This basically means that you will be testing the viability of your system by studying its performance in the past.

After conducting backtesting, it will be important to ensure that your trading strategy can deal with simulations and different market conditions. The system should perform well in all market conditions.

You also need to make sure that your system will perform well when tested against other similar trading strategies. By comparing this performance, you will know whether or not your system works well in the future, just like it did in the past. Backtesting will also allow you to identify and understand your trading strategy’s strengths, weaknesses, advantages, and disadvantages. By doing this, you will know what to change as you begin trading live.

It is not enough for a trader to just be confident in their strategy, but it is equally important that they have studied their system extensively before actually placing a trade with it. By making sure that your system can meet these two requirements, you will be able to find out if it will work in the future just like it did in the past. This is a surefire way to improve your trading performance.

## Chapter 5

 

## Risk to Reward Ratio  
— The King of Strategies

![](Images/image00317.jpeg)

Why is your risk to reward ratio important when trading and investing in cryptocurrency? The risk to reward ratio is a key concept in trading and investing, used to quantify the chance of an investment losing money against the chance of a successful trade. A large or unfavorable risk to reward ratio may make an investment not worth doing, while a high or favorable ratio can make it worthwhile. You might think that they don’t need this metric because cryptocurrency trading and investing are new markets with no historical data to base a risk to reward calculation on. This is true, but it doesn’t take the risk to reward metrics less important. On the contrary, because crypto trading is so new and volatile, traders can benefit from balancing their trades with a favorable risk to reward ratio even more than they would in more established markets.

![](Images/image00352.jpeg)

Defining Risk

To start this discussion, we have to define risk. Risk refers to the financial risks of a trade or investment. These include but are not limited to:

*   Losing All of Your Money: This is the biggest risk, of course, and the one you want to avoid. The best way to protect against this is through diversification, which we’ll discuss more in a later section.
*   Losing Part of Your Money: There is always a chance that you could survive a coin’s market crash relatively unscathed and lose just some of your investment, rather than all.
*   Missing Out on the Upside of a Trade: If you don’t make the most of a successful entry, you could miss out on all the profits. This can be mitigated by using stop losses, but not eliminated.

Risk is not necessarily binary, where risk is all or nothing. For example, you might lose 50% of your investment in a trade with an unfavorable risk to reward ratio but still make money overall.

How to Calculate Risk to Reward Ratio

The decision on whether to enter a trade is not normally based on the risk to reward ratio alone. One of the best things about cryptocurrency trading is that it allows traders to consider more than one metric when making their decision. This makes risk and rewards analysis a useful tool for making trading decisions and determining what type of trades you should be in and when. There are many different types of trades that you can enter, some as obvious as buying and HODL (investing), others more nuanced like day trading. But however you decide to trade, risk and reward analysis is one way you can use the metric to make your decision.

To calculate the risk to reward ratio, we need to consider how much money we are risking for the chance of returning a greater amount. This works in a similar way to the P/E ratio of a stock, where we calculate the earnings per share (EPS) of a company against the price per share that it sells for. If a stock is priced at 10 and has an EPS of 20, you would divide the E to find 1 or divide the P to find 10. Now let’s apply this principle to an investment. We calculate the risk and reward ratio for each investment we make.

To start, you will need to find the price of an investment in dollars or your currency of choice. For example, let’s say your entry cost is 1% of the coin’s value and you are trading Bitcoin (BTC) at $8,000 per coin. In that case, the entry cost is $80.We can now calculate the risk to reward ratio:

(1-(1%)) / 100=$0.00008

This means that an investment of 1 Bitcoin is worth 8% of the price you would lose in that trade. This means that you would lose 8% of $8,000 to make that transaction. Therefore, to make this trade, you would need the opportunity of return of 80% to compensate for the losses you would suffer. Again, it is important to note that this is a very rough approximation and does not necessarily reflect reality.

Now let’s say that 10 BTC costs $8,000 at the time of your trade. In this case, we can see that each coin is worth $800. This means that you would stand to lose $800 in the trade, so you would need an investment return of 800% to make it worthwhile.

This is where the importance of diversification comes into play. For example, suppose your portfolio had a risk to reward ratio of 1:10. In that case, you could afford to make 10 risky investments (like buying multiple cryptocurrencies) and still stand a high chance of being okay. The opposite is true for a lower ratio, like 10:1. In this case, you would need to make more conservative investments and avoid trading altogether if you wanted to maintain your portfolio’s value.

In the example above, we calculated the risk to reward ratio based on 1% of the investment. But because crypto market data is so volatile, it can be difficult to get accurate cost information. One way to adjust for this is to use the average of your recent trades. For example, if you only trade Bitcoin and today it was $8,000, you would multiply the price by 1% to make your entry cost.

Factors Affecting Risk to Reward Ratio

However, as mentioned above, multiple factors can impact your risk to reward ratio when trading cryptocurrencies. Let’s look at some of these factors:

*   Liquidity: This is a big one and one of the main reasons traders choose fiat over cryptocurrency as an investment vehicle. While most of the major cryptocurrencies have high enough liquidity to make investment decisions easier, some like Ripple (XRP) and Bitcoin Cash (BCH) are more thinly traded. This makes these investments more volatile and, in turn, riskier.
*   Market Capitalization: As the market value of a coin increases, so does its overall volatility. This means that it will be riskier to invest in a currency with a higher market capitalization than one with a lower one. The tradeoffs are similar to the idea of diversification.
*   Timeframe: The timeframe you choose for your investment is another factor that can significantly impact the risk to reward ratio. Longer-term investments (holding) often have lower reward potential but less risk. Short-term trades have a higher reward potential but more risk. It’s important to know that the time period you are investing in is one of the main factors that will impact your return on investment.

How to Calculate Risk to Reward Ratio for Altcoins

When it comes to altcoins, the risk-reward ratio is different than that of Bitcoin. But not necessarily by a large degree. If a coin has a high market capitalization, low trading volumes, and a high price, this can all work against it when calculating the risk to reward ratio. The truth is that investors need to be aware ofmany variables when factoring in cryptocurrency returns for trading purposes before they invest in any cryptocurrency.

![](Images/image00353.jpeg)

The risk to reward ratio for an altcoin is calculated in a very similar fashion as it is for Bitcoin. This means that the price of the coin is your entry cost, and you are risking that amount to make a return by trading it. The big difference with altcoins vs. Bitcoin is their underlying value relative to the total market capitalization. While Bitcoin has a high price but low market capitalization, altcoins have a low price but high market capitalization. Because of this, altcoins have a much lower risk to reward ratio than that of Bitcoin.

For example, if you are trading Ethereum (ETH) with an entry cost of $15 and worth $150 at the time of your trade, you would be risking 3% to make a profit. This is because the price of ETH is low relative to its market capitalization. It has a risk to reward ratio of 1:10 because your cost is 3% of the price of ETH. In this example, you would be making a profit of $60 for every $15 you put in. In contrast, if you are trading Ethereum (ETH) with an entry cost of $2.5 and worth $125 at the time of your trade, you would be risking only 1% to make a profit. This is because ETH is high relative to its market capitalization. It has a risk to reward ratio of 1:10 because your cost is 1% of the price of ETH. In this example, you would make a profit of $125 for every $2.5 you buy in the trade.

These factors play into how traders decide to invest their money and form one aspect of many different strategies that are used when trading cryptocurrencies. In the example above, you can also see that a difference in the price of altcoins relative to cash on hand is the one-way traders can make their trading decisions. It is important to note that adding a percentage to cash on hand won’t always accurately assess how risky your investment is relative to the overall market value. This is because many altcoins are very thinly traded, and the majority of them are not valued anywhere near their market capitalization.

In the example above, you can see that adding $5,000 to cash on hand would mean that you would be trading a very low-risk investment, but your cost would likely still be too high. This is because Bitcoin is worth more than $4,000 at the time of your trade, and altcoins are each worth over $1,000. As a result, you could trade with a much lower risk to reward ratio if you had a better understanding of how much risk is involved in the overall market. It is important to note that there are also limits for trading on each cryptocurrency exchange, so you can’t use all of the money you have in your account.

While this strategy can be an effective way to make money trading altcoins, it is important to understand why it works and what kind of risks may be involved. For example, if Bitcoin were to drop in price relative to the rest of the market, your altcoin trades would likely lose value. Suppose you invest in altcoins that are low relative to their market capitalization. In that case, it is important to make sure that the value isn’t just being inflated by people who have faith in what is happening with Bitcoin. This can be a very risky investment.

When it comes to cryptocurrency trading, you will always want to compare the risk-reward ratio of your chosen coin with others out there currently for trading purposes and for long-term investments. It’s important to consider all of the factors involved when evaluating risk vs. reward. If you find that you can make a good return with a low risk to reward ratio, you will be more likely to keep doing it in the long run. If you find that the opposite is true, then you may want to switch things up a bit so that you can eke out a better return.

Spotting High Risk to Reward Ratio Opportunities

When trading cryptocurrency, how do you find trades with high risk to reward ratios? Trading cryptocurrency is not easy. One of the best ways to find trades with high risk to reward ratios is to look at volume. A good rule of thumb is that a trade with a lot more volume than its moving average has the potential for a big move in either direction. The opposite can also be true if you are looking for trades with low risk and low reward, trading lower market cap coins with less volatility but much less profit potential.

One good way to find trades like this is to use multiple time frames. Look for trades with at least 7-10x more volume than the average on a daily chart. For example, if there are 1 million XRP traded per day, and the average is 5,000 XRP, then that is one trade of 1 million XRP at an average price of $500 per coin would be looking at a potential profit of $1 million USD if it went up 100x in a day and it was not filled. Another good way to find a trade like this is by using Fibonacci retracements. For example, one can look for trades that make a Fibonacci retracement of the prior 3-5 days on the daily chart. This helps to define the high-risk traders and the risks they are willing to take while turning off volatility-driven traders. Trading this way helps to filter out the noise and find real signals.

Tactics to Employ for High Risk to Reward Ratio Trades

Catch ‘em With Their Pants Down: What sort of opportunity is this? How can you find trades that are being missed by the market? There are always opportunities that are missed by the market. One of the best ways to find these opportunities is to use a multiple time frame strategy. For example, on a daily chart, look for trades that occur on multiple shorter time frames such as 1 hour, 4 hours, or 6-hour charts. This helps to identify trading opportunities that are being missed by the market as well as identify potential false breakouts. This can also be used to find trade with high risk/high reward trades.

The Short Squeeze: The strategy for finding short squeeze trades often works best when looking at a shorter time frame, such as the hour and half hour charts or even the minute and 30-minute charts. For example, if Bitcoin price is up 20x during a day and it goes down 5% in a few hours and then starts to show signs of a reversal pattern, it will often cause the last traders holding short positions to close their positions. If a sell-off occurs after this short squeeze happens, the trade means a lot more. A good strategy to find these trades is to look for trades that are trading below the prior lows and then rally above the prior highs in a pullback or correction. This helps to identify a potential short squeeze once it happens.

Volatility Opportunities: On the short side of a market, how can you find trades with high risk? Consider looking at lower liquidity coins that are more volatile. This helps to filter out the noise and find real signals. Also, look for coins with low volume that can be traded directly against Bitcoin or other major coins like Ethereum or Litecoin. For example, if you are trading a $25 coin that trades 10k per day and is trading against Bitcoin, it will be harder to see a significant move than if it were trading against the dollar. This helps to filter out the noise while looking for significant moves in the direction of the coin you are trading.

In conclusion, there are a lot of strategies for finding trades with huge potential, with minimal risk and maximum gain, providing you size your positions correctly. One would be to look for coins with low volume that have either unusual price actions or long time frames. This helps to find coins that many traders overlook. In terms of trade ideas, there are many ways one can find trades. For example, traders have found that they can filter their analysis based on a few key indicators such as the moving averages, MACD, and the Lagging Indicators (MA-MLI) when looking at price action or volume to find possible signals.

## Chapter 6

 

## Fundamental and Technical Analysis

![](Images/image00317.jpeg)

In this chapter, we’re going to take a look at everything you need to know about what fundamental and technical analyses are and how you can use them to invest in cryptocurrency profitably.

![](Images/image00354.jpeg)

Fundamental Analysis

This is the driving force behind why the price of a cryptocurrency moves the way it does. The “what” informs the decisions you make about which cryptocurrency is worth buying and holding and which one you should sell because it’s likely at its top. It lets you know who’s behind a coin, the goal, the use case, and the potential, so you know if it’s worth parking some of your investment capital.

The reason fundamental analysis is important is that it can help you forecast prices over the long term, letting you know if the price the crypto is currently trading at is a true reflection of its actual value. For instance, many firmly believe that Ripple’s XRP is heavily underpriced because of the coin’s utility. Therefore, long-term investors would be interested in buying and holding, while short-term traders will keep it in the back of their mind that there’s an upcoming bull run, and there’s a chance they might get burned if they keep shorting the coin.

To learn about a crypto asset’s fundamentals, you should look at the following key factors:

*   The coin’s use case or utility
*   How likely it is that it will be widely adopted in the future
*   The people behind the coin’s development
*   The whitepaper that clarifies what tech the coin is running on and how it will be implemented
*   The coin offering
*   How much the developers of the crypto asset will hold back
*   The coin’s market capitalization or “market cap.”
*   How much volume of this crypto is traded daily
*   Current news events surrounding the asset as well as its developers

Put together, all of these things make up the fundamentals of the coin and can let you know how your investment may work out for you in the coming weeks and months. You should always follow up on the news about your assets in the same way you’d keep up with your preferred soccer team.
